The Great Lexicon of Distinctions (Mahāvyutpatti)

This text is the Great Lexicon of Distinctions, known in Sanskrit as the Mahāvyutpatti and in Tibetan as Jedrak tu Tokpar Jepa Chenpo, a Sanskrit and Tibetan glossary that sets out thousands of Buddhist terms and phrases, each Sanskrit word paired with its approved Tibetan equivalent. It is generally understood to have been compiled in Tibet in the early ninth century by Indian and Tibetan scholars working under royal sponsorship, so that translators of the scriptures would render each technical term in one consistent way. Opening with homage to the Buddha Śākyamuni, it proceeds through thematic sections, beginning with the epithets, bodies and greatness of the Buddha and moving on through doctrinal categories and the names of persons, places and things, and ending with the technical vocabulary of monastic discipline and a list of illnesses. It is Toh 4346 in the Tengyur, where it stands among the works on language and lexicography, and it remains a basic reference for anyone reading Buddhist texts in Sanskrit or Tibetan.

namo bhagavate śākyamunaye tathāgatāyārhate samyaksaṃbuddhaya.

Names for the synonyms of the Buddha's epithets, bodies and greatness: buddhaḥ, bhagavān.

Homage to the Blessed One, the Tathāgata, the Arhat, the perfectly complete Buddha Śākyamuni.

Buddha. Blessed One.

tathāgataḥ, arhan, samyaksaṃbuddhaḥ, vidyācaraṇasampannaḥ, sugataḥ, lokavitra, anuttarapuruṣadamyasārathiḥ.

Tathāgata. Arhat, perfectly complete Buddha. Endowed with knowledge and conduct. Sugata. Knower of the world. Unsurpassed charioteer who tames persons.

Names of the eighteen unshared qualities of the Buddha:

Fearlessness in knowing the exhaustion of all defilements. Fearlessness in declaring with certainty that the obstructive dharmas are not otherwise. Fearlessness in this: that the path of deliverance, by which all excellences are attained, is just so.

Names for the greatness of the Tathāgata found in the sūtras:

subiśuddhabuddhiḥ, advayasamudācāraḥ, alakṣaṇadharmmaparāyaṇaḥ, buddhabihāreṇa biharaṃ, sarbbabuddhasamatāprāptaḥ, anābaraṇagatiṃ gataḥ, apratyudābartyadharmmaḥ

Mind perfectly pure. Free of dualistic conduct. Intent on the Dharma that is without defining characteristics. Abiding in the abiding of the buddhas. Having attained sameness with all buddhas. Having come to realization through being free of obscurations. Endowed with irreversible qualities.

asaṃharyagocaraḥ, acintyabyabasthāna, tryadhvasamatāniryātaḥ, sarbalokadhātuprabhūtakāyaḥ, sarbadharmmaniḥsaṃśayajñānaḥ, sarbacaryāsamanbāgatabuddhiḥ, niṣkāṃkṣodharmmajñāne

Whose sphere of activity cannot be wrested away. Whose ordering of things is inconceivable. Having fully arrived at the sameness of the three times. Whose body pervades all world systems. Possessing wisdom free of doubt about all phenomena. Whose mind is endowed with every kind of conduct. Free of all wavering in knowing phenomena.

abikalpitaśarīraṃḥ, sarbabodhisatvasaṃpratīcchajñānaḥ, advayabuddhabihāraparamapāramitaprāptaḥ, asaṃbhinnatathāgatabimokṣajñānaniṣṭhāgataḥ, anantamadhya

Possessing a body that is not conceptually constructed. Whose wisdom is fully received by all bodhisattvas. Having attained the supreme perfection of the nondual abiding of the buddhas. Having reached the culmination of the wisdom of the unmixed activity of the tathāgatas.

buddhabhūmisamatādhigataḥ, dharmmadhātuparyantaḥ, ākāśadhātuparyabasānaḥ, aparāntakoṭiniṣṭhaḥ, niṣyandaḥ s tathāgataḥ puṇyānāṃ, abipraṇāśaḥ kuśalamūlānāṃ, alaṃkṛtaḥ kṣāntyā, ālayaḥ puṇyani

Having realized the sameness of the buddha ground, which has neither end nor middle. Reaching to the very limit of the dharmadhātu. Ending only where the element of space ends. Reaching to the furthest limit of the future. That Tathāgata is the natural outflow of merit. His roots of virtue are never wasted. Adorned with patience.

dhānānāṃ, citrito'nubyañjanaiḥ, kusumitolakṣaṇaiḥ, pratīrūpogocareṇa, sapratikūlodarśanina, abhiratiḥ śrāddhādhimuktānāṃ, anbhibhūtaḥ prajñayā, anabamardanīyo balaiḥ, śāstā

The ground of the treasuries of merit. Embellished with the excellent minor marks. Blossoming with the flowers of the major marks. Whose sphere of activity is fitting and harmonious. In whom there is nothing displeasing to behold. The delight of those devoted through faith. Unconquerable in wisdom. Invincible in his powers.

sarbasatvānāṃ, pitābodhisatvānāṃ, rājā āryāpuṅgalānāṃ, sārthābāha ādikarmikānāṃ, aprameyo jñānena, anantaḥ pratibhānina, biśuddhassvareṇa, sāsvādanīyo ghoṣeṇa, asecanako rūpeṇa, apratisamaḥ kā

The Teacher of all sentient beings. Father of the bodhisattvas. King of the noble persons. Caravan leader of beginners. Immeasurable in wisdom. Infinite in eloquence. Speech utterly pure. Voice melodious. A form one never tires of beholding.

yena, aliptaḥ kāmaiḥ, anupalipto rūpaiḥ, asaṃsṛṣṭa ārūpyaiḥ, bipramuktaḥ skandhebhyaḥ, bisaṃyukto dhātubhiḥ, saṃbṛtta āyatanaiḥ, pracchinno granthaiḥ, bimuktaḥ bridāghaiḥ, pari

A body without equal. Unstained by the realms of desire. Untainted by the realms of form. Unmingled with the formless realms. Utterly liberated from the aggregates. Disjoined from the elements. The sense sources restrained. The knots completely cut. Liberated from all torments.

muktatṛṣṇayā, aughāduttīrṇṇaḥ, paripūrṇṇo jñānena, pratiṣṭhito'tītānāgatapratyutpannāṃ buddhānāṃ bhagabatāṃ jñāne, apratiṣṭhito nirbāṇe, sthito bhūtakoṭyāṃ, sthitaḥ sarbasatvollokanī

Completely freed from craving. Crossed over the floods. Wisdom perfectly complete. Abiding in the wisdom of the buddhas, the blessed ones, of the past, the future, and the present. Not abiding in nirvāṇa. Abiding at the very limit of reality.

yāyāṃ bhūmau, anantaśiṣyagaṇasubinītaḥ, sarbasatvāśayasubidhijñāḥ, anābhogabuddhakāryapratiprasrabdhaḥ, muktomuktabaribāraḥ, pāragataḥ, sthalagataḥ

Dwelling on the ground of beholding all sentient beings. Possessing an infinite host of well-disciplined disciples. Knowing well how to fulfill the intentions of all sentient beings. His effortless buddha activity never ceasing. Liberated, with a liberated retinue. Gone to the far shore. Arrived on dry land.

tīrṇṇaḥ, tārakaḥ, āśvastaḥ, śotriyaḥ, bhinnakleśaḥ, bāhitapābadharmmā, bedakaḥ, binībaraṇaḥ, biprasannamanaḥ, ṣaḍaṃgasamanvāgataḥ, ananuroddhabiroddhabibramuktaḥ, praṇantapratyakasatyaiḥ, samutsṛṣṭaiṣaṇaḥ

liptaḥ, sarbamalāpagataḥ, candraprabhaḥ, sūryaprabhaḥ, jñānaprabhaḥ, bhadrapālaḥ, meruśikharadhārakumārabhūtaḥ, baruṇamatikumārabhūtaḥ, sumatikumārabhūtaḥ, nityodyuktaḥ, susārthabāhaḥ, jyotiṣmatikumārabhūtaḥ, durdharṣa

Anupalipta. Sarvamalāpagata. Candraprabha. Sūryaprabha. Jñānaprabha. Bhadrapāla. Meruśikharadhara the Youthful. Varuṇamati the Youthful. Sumati the Youthful. Nityodyukta. Susārthavāha. Jyotiṣmat the Youthful.

kumārabhūtaḥ, gaganagañjaḥ, śūraṃgamaḥ, akṣayamatiḥ, pratibhānakuṭaḥ, gandhahastiḥ, jālinīprabhaḥ, barddhamānamatiḥ, samantabrabhaḥ, ādityagarbhaḥ, sarbabiṣayābabhāsālaṃkārapratibhānadarśanagarbhaḥ

Durdharṣa the Youthful. Gaganagañja. Śūraṃgama. Akṣayamati. Pratibhānakūṭa. Gandhahastin. Jālinīprabha. Vardhamānamati. Samantaprabha. Ādityagarbha: where it will not be confused with Sūrya it is rendered Essence of the Sun, and where it would be, Essence of the Sun Disk. Sarvaviṣayāvabhāsālaṃkārapratibhānadarśanagarbha.

amalagarbhaḥ, bimalagarbhaḥ, jyotijvalanārcciśrīgarbhaḥ, bajrasāraḥ, nityaprabhaḥ, āśuggandhaḥ, guhaguptaḥ, amoghadarśī, susaṃprosthitaḥ, anikṣiptadhuraḥ, anupahatamatiḥ, nityotkṣiptahastaḥ

Amalagarbha. Vimalagarbha. Jyotijvalanārciśrīgarbha. Vajrasāra. Nityaprabha. Āśugandha. Guhagupta. Amoghadarśin. Susaṃprasthita. Anikṣiptadhura. Anupahatamati. Nityotkṣiptahasta.

nadīdattā, bijayabikrāmī, jayadattaḥ, bigataśokaḥ, bhadrakalpikabodhisatvaḥ, anyecamahojaskabodhisatvaḥ, jagatindharaḥ, jyotiḥprabhaḥ, ekakāntarājā, bhūmibalabaiśāradya

Nadīdatta. Vijayavikrāmin. Jayadatta. Vigataśoka. The bodhisattvas of the Fortunate Eon. And other bodhisattvas of great splendor as well. Jagatīṃdhara. Jyotiḥprabha, rendered either Starlight or Firelight. Ekakāntarāja.

dhāri, sucandraḥ, aparājitatejaḥ, acintikamadhyabuddhibikrīḍitaḥ, jñānabibhūtigarbhaḥ, buddhabajrasandhāraṇasandhiḥ

Bhūmibalavaiśāradyadhārin. Sucandra. Aparājitatejas. Acintikamadhyabuddhivikrīḍita. Jñānavibhūtigarbha. Buddhavajrasandhāraṇasandhi.

Names of the samādhis of bodhisattvas:

ratnasamuṅgataḥ, supratiṣṭhaḥ, akambyaḥ, a

Jewel Fully Exalted. Firmly Established. Unstirred.

binibarttanīyaḥ, ratnākaraḥ, sūryaprabhatejaḥ, sarbārthasiddhaḥ, jñānolkaḥ, pratyutpannabuddhaḥsaṃmukhābasthitaḥ

Irreversible. Ratnākara. Sūryaprabhatejas. Accomplishing All Aims. Lamp of Wisdom. Standing Face to Face with the Buddhas of the Present.

Names of the twelve dhāraṇīs of bodhisattvas:

abhiṣecabatīḥ biśuddhasvaranighoṣā, akṣayakaraṇḍā, anantābarttā, sāgaramudrā

Lotus Array. Entering the Door of Non-attachment. Entering Certainty in the Analytical Knowledges. Blessed with the Ornaments of the Buddhas. Infinite Colors. Bringing Forth the Perfect Complexion of a Buddha's Body.

Names of the ten powers of bodhisattvas:

āśayabalaṃ, adhyāśayabalaṃ

The power of intention. The power of superior intention.

prayogabalaṃ, jñānabalaṃ, praṇidhānabalaṃ, yānabalaṃ, caryabalaṃ, bikurbbaṇbalaṃ, bodhibalaṃ, dharmacakraprabarttanabalaṃ

The power of application. The power of wisdom. The power of aspiration. The power of the vehicle. The power of conduct. The power of miraculous display. The power of awakening. The power of turning the wheel of Dharma.

Names of the ten masteries of bodhisattvas:

āyurbaśitā, cittabaśitā, pariskārabaśitā, karmmabaśitā, upa

Mastery over life span. Mastery over mind. Mastery over requisites. Mastery over karma.

pattibaśitā, adhimuktibaśitā, dharmmabaśitā, praṇidhānabaśitā, ṛddhibaśitā, jñānabaśitā

Mastery over birth. Mastery over inclination. Mastery over the Dharma. Mastery over aspiration. Mastery over miraculous powers. Mastery over wisdom.

Names of the four fearlessnesses of bodhisattvas:

dhāraṇīśrutoṅgrahaṇārthanirdeśabaiśāradyaṃ, nairatmyādhigamātparabiheṭhanānimittasamudācārasahajānadhi

Fearlessness in retaining through dhāraṇī what has been heard, and in teaching its meaning.

giteryāpathatrikarmma, pariśuddhamahārakṣasaṃpannabaiśāradyaṃ, sadotgṛhitadharmābismaraṇaprajñopāyaniṣṭhāgatasatvanistāraṇaprasādasandarśanaśubhānantarāyikabaiśāradyaṃ, sarbajñātācittāsaṃ

The fearlessness of this: because selflessness has been realized, no sign of harm from others arises, one's conduct is by nature beyond reproach, and one is endowed with the great protection of the three kinds of action fully purified. Fearlessness in never forgetting the Dharma one has taken up, in freeing sentient beings and displaying what inspires faith through having perfected skillful means and wisdom, and in never becoming an obstacle to virtue.

pramoṣānyayānāniryāṇasaṃpūrṇabasitāsarbabrakārasatvārthasaṃprāpaṇabaiśāradyaṃ

Fearlessness in never losing the mind set on omniscience, in perfecting mastery without going forth by any other vehicle, and in fully accomplishing the welfare of sentient beings in every way.

Names of the eighteen unshared qualities of bodhisattvas:

anupadiṣṭadānāḥ, anupadiṣṭaśīlāḥ, anupadiṣṭa

Those whose generosity is untaught. Those whose moral discipline is untaught.

kṣāntayaḥ, anupadiṣṭabīryāḥ, anupadiṣṭadhyānāḥ, anupadiṣṭaprajñāḥ, saṃgrahabastusarbasatvasaṃgrāhakāḥ, pariṇāmanabidhijñāḥ, upāyakauśalyasarbasatvacaritā

Those whose patience is untaught. Those whose diligence is untaught. Those whose meditative concentration is untaught. Those whose wisdom is untaught. Those who gather all sentient beings through the means of attraction. Those who know the rite of dedication.

baśitaparamayānaniryāṇasandarśakāḥ, mahāyānācyutāḥ, saṃsāranirbāṇamukhasandarśakāḥ, yamakabyatyastāhārakuśalāḥ, jñānabūrbaṃgamanābhisaṃskāranirbadyaṃsarbajanmābhimukhabra

Those who, through skill in means and mastery of the conduct of all sentient beings, go forth by the supreme vehicle. Those who never fall away from the Great Vehicle. Those who show the doors of saṃsāra and nirvāṇa. Those skilled in sequences of pairs and of crosswise arrangements.

bṛttāḥ, daśakuśalopetakāyabāgmanskarmāntāḥ, sarbaduḥkhskandhasahānātmopādānasarbasatvadhātvaparityāginaḥ, sarbajagadbhirucitasaṃdarśakāḥ, kiyatkṛcchabā

Those who, with wisdom going before, without deliberate formation and without fault, enter manifestly into all their lifetimes. Those whose actions of body, speech, and mind are endowed with the ten virtues. Those who never forsake any realm of sentient beings, taking on bodies that bear every mass of suffering. Those who display what delights all beings.

laśrābakamadhyaśubhabyūharatnakalpabṛkṣadṛḍhasarbajñatācittāsaṃpramuṣitāḥ, sarbadharmapaṭṭābaddhābhiṣekaprāptibuddhadharmmaparyeṣṭisandarśanānibṛttāḥ

The qualities of bodhisattvas found in the sūtras. Those whose mind of omniscience, steadfast like a jewel wish-fulfilling tree of manifold virtue, never deteriorates, even in the midst of however many terrible ones. Those who never turn back from displaying the search for the qualities of a buddha, in order to attain empowerment over all phenomena through the bestowal of means.

Names mentioned at the outset:

ekajātipratibaddhaḥ, sarbajñātābhimukhaḥ, sarbajñātānimnaḥ, sarbajñātāprabaṇaḥ, sarbajñātāprāgbhāraḥ, asaṃgadhāraṇīsamādhipratilabdhaḥ, śūraṃgamasamādhisamanvāgataḥ

Bound to only one more birth. Facing omniscience. Inclined toward omniscience. Flowing toward omniscience. Bent toward omniscience. Having attained unattached dhāraṇī and meditative absorption. Endowed with the Śūraṃgama samādhi.

mahābhijñābikṝḍitaḥ, sarbābaraṇaparyutthānabigataḥ, apratiprasrabdhamārgaḥ, mahāmaitrīmahākaruṇādaśadiglokadhātuspharaṇaḥ, anantabuddhakṣetrākramaṇakuśalaḥ, śū

Sporting with the great superknowledges. Free of all obscurations, impediments, and active afflictions. Whose path is uninterrupted. Pervading the world systems of the ten directions with great loving-kindness and compassion. Skilled in going to boundless buddha fields.

nyatāgocaraḥ, animittabihāriḥ, sarbapraṇidhānaniśrayabigataḥ, sarbasatvahitābhyudyataḥ, sarbabuddhabiṣayakuśalaḥ, anantajñānaḥ, ākāśasamacittaḥ, sāgarabaṅgambhīracittaḥ, sume

Whose domain is emptiness. Dwelling in signlessness. Free of all dwelling in aspirations. Striving for the benefit of all sentient beings. Skilled in all the domains of the buddhas. Of boundless wisdom. Whose mind is like space. Whose mind is deep like the ocean.

rubarbatarājabadakampyacittaḥ, padmabadanupaliptacittaḥ, ratnabatsupariśuddhacittaḥ, subarṇṇabatsuparyabdātacittaḥ, aparimitajñānasaṃbhāraparyeṣaṇkuśalaḥ, paraprabādyanbhidhūtaḥ, sarbadharmānābaraṇajñānī

Whose mind is unshakable like Sumeru, king of mountains. Whose mind is unstained like a lotus. Whose mind is utterly pure like a jewel. Whose mind is thoroughly refined like gold. Skilled in seeking the immeasurable accumulation of wisdom. Not overpowered by the challenges of opponents. Possessing wisdom unobstructed with regard to all phenomena.

sarbasatvasamacittaḥ, sarbamārabiṣayasamatikrāntaḥ, sarbatathāgatabaṣayābatārajñānakuśalaḥ, mahāmaitrīmahākaruṇāsamanbāgataḥ, ubāyajñānakuśalaḥ, dhāraṇīpratilabdhaḥ, praṇibhānākalpitaḥ, kṣānti

Whose mind is impartial toward all sentient beings. Having wholly transcended all the domains of Māra. Skilled in the knowledge that enters the domain of all the tathāgatas. Endowed with great loving-kindness and great compassion. Skilled in the knowledge of skillful means. Having attained dhāraṇī. Whose aspirations are free of conceptual contrivance.

samatāpratilabdhaḥ, acyutābhijñaḥ, nirāmiṣadharmadeśakaḥ, gaṃbhīradharmakṣāntipāramiṃgataḥ, mārakarmmasamatikrāntaḥ, karmābaraṇapratiprasrabdhaḥ, dharmmabrabicayabibhaktinirdeśakuśalaḥ, asaṃ

Having attained sameness through patience. Whose superknowledges never decline. Teaching the Dharma without worldly gain. Having reached the far shore of patience toward the profound Dharma. Having wholly transcended the deeds of Māra. Having cut off the stream of karmic obscuration. Skilled in teaching the distinctions through thorough discernment of phenomena.

khyeyakalpapraṇidhānasusamārambhaḥ, smitamukhapūrbābhilāpiḥ, gāthābhigītābhilāpanaḥ, apagatalīnacittaḥ, anācchedyapratibhānaḥ, anantabariṣadabhibhabanaḥ, anantakalpakoṭīniḥ, saraṇakuśalaḥ, māyā

Having well undertaken aspirations for countless eons. Speaking openly, with a smiling face. Speaking in the melody of verse. Whose mind is free of faintheartedness. Whose eloquence never ceases. Outshining boundless assemblies. Skilled in emerging from endless tens of millions of eons.

marīcikodakacandrasvapnapratiśrūtkāpratibhāsapratibimbanirmmāṇopamadharmmādhimuktaḥ, apratihatacittaḥ, satvacittacaritasūkṣmajñānādhimuktyabatārakuśalaḥ, adhimātrakṣāntisamanvāgataḥ, yathātmyābatārakuśalaḥ

Having conviction in phenomena as being like an illusion, a mirage, the moon in water, a dream, an echo, an optical illusion, a reflection, and a magical emanation. Whose mind is unimpeded. Skilled in entering, with conviction, into the subtle knowledge of the minds and conduct of sentient beings. Endowed with great patience. Skilled in entering into things just as they are.

buddhakṣetrabyūhānantapraṇidhānaprasthānaparigṛhītaḥ, asaṃkhyeyalokadhātubuddhānusmṛtisamādhisatatasamitaabhimukhībhūtaḥ, aparimitabuddhādhyeṣaṇakuśalaḥ, nānādṛṣṭyānuśaya

Sustained by setting out on aspirations for boundless arrays of buddha fields. In whom the meditative absorption of recollecting the buddhas of countless world systems is constantly and continuously present. Skilled in making requests of immeasurable buddhas.

paryābasthānakleśapraśamanakuśalaḥ, samādhibikrīḍitaśatasahasranirhārakuśalaḥ, sarbajñatānirlātaḥ, traidhātukāsaktaḥ, gatiṃgataḥ, sarbāśāparipūrakaḥ, apramāṇasamādhi

Skilled in thoroughly pacifying the various views, the latent tendencies, and the entangling afflictions. Skilled in accomplishing hundreds of thousands of plays of meditative absorption. Going forth toward omniscience. Unattached to the three realms. Having fully penetrated through realization. Fulfilling all hopes.

samāpattiḥsmanvāgataḥ, amoghakāyabāgmānaskarmmāntābhiyuktaḥ, apramāṇasamādhicaryādhiṣṭhitaḥ, napunarbbodhisatvamahāsatvaḥkāmaguṇairliptaḥ, sarbasamādhibaśitāpāramiṃgataḥ, anu

Endowed with immeasurable meditative absorptions and attainments. Diligent in making the actions of body, speech, and mind bear fruit. Empowered by an immeasurable conduct of meditative absorption. Bodhisattva great beings unstained by the qualities of sense desire. Having reached the far shore of mastery over all meditative absorptions.

paliptolokadharmaiḥ, yuktapratibhānaḥ, muktapratibhānaḥ, maitryātmakaḥ, karuṇātmakaḥ, muditābihārī, upekṣābihārī, acyutaśīlaḥ, acyutasamādhiḥ, abhijñābikrīḍitaḥ, tecabodhisatvamahā

Unstained by the worldly concerns. Of apt eloquence. Of free-flowing eloquence. Whose very nature is loving-kindness. Whose very nature is compassion. Dwelling in joy. Dwelling in equanimity. Whose moral discipline never declines. Whose meditative absorption never declines. Sporting with the superknowledges.

satvabhūyastenasarbakumārabhūtāḥ, bhadrakalpikabodhisatvaḥ.

Names of the ground of conduct through conviction: adhimukti

And all of those bodhisattva great beings were, for the most part, in the state of youth. The bodhisattvas of the Fortunate Eon (Bhadrakalpa). The Supremely Joyful. The Stainless. The Luminous. The Radiant. The Very Hard to Master. The Manifest. The Gone Far. The Immovable. Good Intelligence. The Cloud of Dharma.

caryābhūmiḥ, ālokalabdhaḥ, ālokabṛdhiḥ, tatvārthaikadeśānuprabeśaḥ, anantaryasmādhiḥ.

Names of the ten Dharma practices on the ground of conduct through conviction: lekhanā, būjanā, dānaṃ, śrabaṇaṃ, bācanā, uṅgrahaṇaṃ, prakāśanā, svādhyāyaḥ, cintanā

bhābanā.

Names of the ten perfections: dānapāramitā, śīlapāramitā, kṣāntipāramitā, bīryapāramitā, dhyānapāramitā, prajñāpāramitā, upāyabāramitā, praṇidhānapāramitā, balapāra

mitā, jñānapāramitā.

Names of the four means of attraction: dānaṃ, priyabāditā, arthacaryā, samānārtha.

Names of the three trainings: adhiśīlaṃ, adhicittaṃ, adhiprajñaṃ.

Names of the eighteen emptinesses: adhyātmaśūnyatā, bahirdhāśūnyatā, adhyātmabahirdhāśūnyatā, śūnyatāśūnyatā

The perfection of power. The perfection of primordial wisdom. Giving. Pleasant speech. Beneficial conduct. Consistency of aims. Higher moral discipline. Higher mind. Higher wisdom. Emptiness of the internal. Emptiness of the external. Emptiness of the external and internal. Emptiness of emptiness.

mahāśūnyatā, paramārthaśūnyatā, saṃskṛtaśūnyatā, asaṃskṛtaśūnyatā, atyantaśūnyatā, anabarāgraśūnyatā, anabakāraśūnyatā, prakṛtiśūnyatā, sarbadharmmaśūnyatā, svalakṣaṇaśūnyatā, anu

Emptiness of the great. Emptiness of the ultimate. Emptiness of the conditioned. Emptiness of the unconditioned. Emptiness of what transcends the extremes. Emptiness of what has neither beginning nor end. Emptiness of what is not to be discarded. Emptiness of intrinsic nature. Emptiness of all phenomena. Emptiness of own characteristics.

palaṃbaśūnyatā, abhābaśūnyatā, svabhābaśūnyatā, abhābasvabhābaśūnyatā.

Names of the four applications of mindfulness: kāyasmṛtyubasthānam, bedansmṛtyubasthānaṃ, cittasmṛtyubasthānaṃ, dharmmasmṛtyubasthānaṃ.

Names of the four right abandonments: anutpannānāṃpāpakā

Emptiness of the unobservable. Emptiness of non-entities. Emptiness of essential nature. Emptiness of the essential nature of non-entities. The application of mindfulness to the body. The application of mindfulness to feelings. The application of mindfulness to the mind. The application of mindfulness to phenomena.

nāmakuśalānāndharmmāṇāmanutpādāyacchandaṃjānayati, utpannānāmpāpakānāmakuśalānāṃdharmmāṇāṃprahāṇāyacchandaṃjānayati, anutpannānāṃkuśalānāndharmmāṇāmutpādāyacchandaṃjānayati, utpannānāmkuśalānāndharmmāṇāṃsthitāyabhūyobhābatāyaiasaṃpramoṣāyaparipūrayecchandaṃjā

One generates aspiration so that evil, unwholesome phenomena that have not arisen do not arise. One generates aspiration in order to abandon evil, unwholesome phenomena that have arisen. One generates aspiration in order to produce wholesome phenomena that have not arisen.

nayati, byāyacchate, bīryamārabhate, cittaṃpragṛhnāti, saṃmyakpradadhāti.

Names of the four bases of miraculous power: cchandasamādhiprahāṇasaṃskārasamanvāgatoṛddhipādaḥ, cittasamādhiprahāṇasaṃskārasamanvāgatoṛddhi

One generates aspiration in order to maintain wholesome phenomena that have arisen, to make them occur again and again, to keep them from declining, and to bring them to completion. One strives. One undertakes diligence. One takes hold of the mind. One sets it rightly. The base of miraculous power endowed with the meditative absorption of aspiration and the formation of abandonment. The base of miraculous power endowed with the meditative absorption of mind and the formation of abandonment.

pādaḥ, bīryasamādhiprahāṇasaṃskārasamanvāgatoṛddhipādaḥ, mīmāṃsasamādhiprahāṇasaṃskārasamanvāgatoṛddhipādaḥ, anupalaṃbhayogenabhābati, bibekaniśritaṃ, birāganiśritaṃ, nirodhaniśri

The base of miraculous power endowed with the meditative absorption of diligence and the formation of abandonment. The base of miraculous power endowed with the meditative absorption of investigation and the formation of abandonment. One cultivates them in a non-objectifying way.

Dwelling in solitude. Dwelling in freedom from attachment. Dwelling in cessation.

taṃ, byabasargapariṇataṃ.

Names of the five faculties: śraddhendriyaṃ, bīryandriyaṃ, smṛtīndriyaṃ, samādhīndriyaṃ, prajñendriyaṃ.

Names of the seven limbs of awakening: smṛ

Transformed through relinquishment. The faculty of faith. The faculty of diligence. The faculty of mindfulness. The faculty of meditative absorption. The faculty of wisdom. The faculty of faith. The power of diligence. The power of mindfulness. The power of meditative absorption. The power of wisdom.

tisaṃbodhyaṃga, dharmmaprabicayasaṃbodhyaṃga, bīryasaṃbodhyaṃgaṃ, prītisaṃbodhyaṃgaṃ, prasrabdhisaṃbodhyaṃgaṃ, samādhisaṃbodhyaṃgaṃ, upekṣa

Mindfulness, a limb of perfect awakening. Thorough discrimination of phenomena, a limb of perfect awakening. Diligence, a limb of perfect awakening. Joy, a limb of perfect awakening. Pliancy, a limb of perfect awakening. Meditative absorption, a limb of perfect awakening.

saṃbodhyaṃgaṃ.

Names of the noble eightfold path: saṃmyagdṛṣṭiḥ, saṃmyaksaṃkalpaḥ, saṃmyagvākra, saṃmyakkarmmāntaḥ, saṃmyakjībaḥ, saṃmyakbyāyāmaḥ, samyaksmṛtiḥ, samyaksamādhiḥ.

Pure conduct. Good in the beginning. Good in the middle. Good in the end. Excellent in meaning. Excellent in wording. Unmixed; in other contexts it is also rendered "exclusive." Complete. Wholly pure. Wholly cleansed. Those who proclaim it proclaim it thus.

The Dharma of the Blessed One is well spoken. It is seen correctly. It is free of sickness. It is not bound by time. It leads onward. It is meaningful to behold. It is to be known by the wise, each for themselves.

The Dharma and Vinaya well spoken by the Blessed One are well taught. They lead to definite emergence and bring one to perfect awakening. They are without discord and possessed of harmony. They have a support. Their Teacher is the Tathāgata, the arhat, the perfectly complete Buddha.

Monks, my Dharma is well spoken. It is clear. It is opened up. The stream is cut. It is rightly and thoroughly taught, as far as gods and humans.

Turning the wheel of Dharma in twelve aspects, repeating it three times. The first round of the noble truths, the path of seeing: This is suffering. This is the origin. This is cessation. This is the path to the cessation of suffering. The second round of the noble truths, the path of meditation: The noble truth of suffering is to be fully understood. The origin of suffering is to be abandoned. The cessation of suffering is to be actualized. The path leading to the cessation of suffering is to be cultivated. The third round of the noble truths, the path of no more learning: Suffering has been fully understood. The origin has been abandoned. Cessation has been actualized. The path leading to the cessation of suffering has been cultivated.

Spoken by the mouth. Scripture. Sacred text. Excellent speech. Teaching, or that which corrects. Sūtra collection. Sūtra. Tenet system. Doctrinal system, or, in the context of mantra, sacred commitment. Instruction. Advice. View. Tradition. Treatise. Exposition. Account. Condensed into aphorisms. Composed in verse. Brief explanation, or outline. Commentary. Explanatory commentary. Commentary on difficult points. Explanation. Detailed explanation. Extensive commentary. Textual commentary. Mixed composition. Subcommentary. Explanation of a subcommentary. Verse. Prose. Metrical composition. Composition. Prosody. Continuous composition. Words, or text, or number of chapters. Chapter. Section. Divided into chapters. Arranged in chapters. Arranged in sequence. Chapter of recitation. Compendium of prosody. Summary of the collection. Intermediate summary. Summary; in some contexts it is also rendered "in brief."

Secluded from desires, secluded from evil and unwholesome phenomena, with conception and with analysis, possessing the joy and bliss born of seclusion, one fully attains and abides in the first meditative concentration. Free of conception and analysis, inwardly wholly serene, and with the mind's continuum become unified, therefore without conception and without analysis, possessing the joy and bliss born of meditative absorption, one fully attains and abides in the second meditative concentration. Because free of attachment to joy, one abides in equanimity, is mindful and vigilant, and experiences bliss with the body; that state of which the noble ones say, "Equanimous and mindful, one abides in bliss," the joyless third meditative concentration, one fully attains and abides in.

Names of the eight liberations: rūpīrūpāṇi baśyaṃtyayaṃ brathamo bimokṣaḥ. adhyātmamarūpasaṃjñābahirdhārūpāṇi paśyaṃtyayaṃ dvitīyo bimokṣaḥ. śubhaṃ bimokṣaṃ kāyena sākṣātkṛtvopasaṃpadya biharatyayaṃ tritīyo bimokṣaḥ. sa sarbbaśo rūpasaṃjñānāṃ samatikramātpratighasaṃjñānāmastaṃgamāt nānātvasaṃjñānāmamanasikārādanantamākāśamityākāśānantyāyatanamupasaṃpadya biharatyayañcaturartho bimokṣaḥ. sa sarbaśa ākāśānantyāyatanaṃ samatikramyānantaṃ bijñānamiti bijñānānantyāyatanasamupasaṃpadya biharatyayaṃ pañcamo bimokṣaḥ. sa sarbaśo bijñānānantyāyatanaṃ samatikramya nāsti kiṃcidityākiṃcityāyatanamupasaṃpadya biharatyayaṃ ṣaṣṭho bimokṣaḥ. sa sarbaśa ākiṃcityāyatanaṃ samatikramya naibasaṃjñānāsaṃjñāyatanamupasaṃpadya biharatyayaṃ saptamo bimokṣaḥ. de rnampaśo naibasaṃjñānāsaṃjñāyatanaṃ samatikramya saṃjñābeditanirodhaṃ kāyena sākṣātkṛtvobasaṃbadya biharatyayamaṣṭhamo bimokṣaḥ.

Names of the eight spheres of mastery: adhyātmaṃ rūpasaṃjñī bahirddhā rūpāṇi baśyanti parittāni su

Having abandoned bliss, and having already abandoned suffering before, and since mental pleasure and mental displeasure have also subsided, one fully attains and abides in the fourth meditative concentration, which is neither bliss nor suffering and is wholly purified by equanimity and mindfulness.

The meditative concentration of the utmost limit. The not-incapable. The distinctive meditative concentration. The threshold stage. The main stage. The space-pervading meditative absorption. Settling correctly within. Placed in evenness. With the mind constantly and uninterruptedly placed in evenness.

Having completely transcended perceptions of form in every way, with perceptions of resistance subsided, and through not attending to perceptions of variety, thinking, "Space is infinite," one fully attains and abides in the sphere of infinite space. Having completely transcended the sphere of infinite space in every way, thinking, "Consciousness is infinite," one fully attains and abides in the sphere of infinite consciousness. Having completely transcended the sphere of infinite consciousness in every way, thinking, "There is nothing at all," one fully attains and abides in the sphere of nothingness. Having transcended the sphere of nothingness in every way, one fully attains and abides in the sphere of neither perception nor non-perception.

The attainment of leaping over. The attainment of leaping over. The nine attainments of successive abiding. The attainment of cessation. Bringing the great elements into harmony. The attainment without perception.

Loving-kindness. Compassion. Joy. Equanimity.

With a mind endowed with loving-kindness, free of enmity, without rival, free of harm, vast, become great, immeasurable, non-dual, and thoroughly cultivated, one resolves upon one direction, pervades it, and fully attains and abides. Likewise the second, likewise the third, likewise the fourth; above, below and across, in every way, this whole world with all it contains.

One with form looks at forms: this is the first liberation. Perceiving oneself inwardly as formless, one looks at outer forms: this is the second liberation. Having directly realized with the body the liberation of the beautiful, one fully attains and abides in it: this is the third liberation. Having completely transcended perceptions of form in every way, with perceptions of resistance subsided, and through not attending to perceptions of variety, thinking, "Space is infinite," one fully attains and abides in the sphere of infinite space: this is the fourth liberation. Having completely transcended the sphere of infinite space in every way, thinking, "Consciousness is infinite," one fully attains and abides in the sphere of infinite consciousness: this is the fifth liberation. Having completely transcended the sphere of infinite consciousness in every way, thinking, "There is nothing at all," one fully attains and abides in the sphere of nothingness: this is the sixth liberation. Having completely transcended the sphere of nothingness in every way, one fully attains and abides in the sphere of neither perception nor non-perception: this is the seventh liberation. Having completely transcended the sphere of neither perception nor non-perception in every way, and having directly realized with the body the cessation of perception and feeling, one fully attains and abides in it: this is the eighth liberation.

barṇṇadurbarṇṇāni tāni khalu rūpāñabhibhūya jānāntyabhibhūya baśyantyebaṃ saṃjñī ca bhabatīdaṃ prathamamabhibhvāyatanaṃ. adhyātmamarūpasaṃjñā bahirdhā rūbāṇi baśyanti mahadgatāni subarṇṇadurbarṇṇāni tāni khalu rūpāṇyabhibhūya jānāntyabhibhūya paśyantyebaṃ saṃjñī ca bhabatīdaṃ dvitīyamabhibhvāyatanaṃ. adhyātmamarūpasaṃjñā bahirdhā rūbāṇi baśyanti nīlāni nīlabarṇṇāni nīlanidarśanāni nīlanirbhāsāni tadyathā umakāpuṣpaṃ sampannaṃ bā bārāṇaseyaṃ bastraṃ nīlaṃ nīlabarṇṇaṃ nīlanidarśanaṃ nīlanirbhāsamebamebādhyātmamarūpasaṃjñī bahirddhā rūpāṇi baśyanti nīlāni nīlabarṇṇāni nīlanidarśanāni nīlanirbhāsānīdaṃ tritīyamabhibhvāyatanam. adhyātmamarūpasaṃjñī bahirdhā rūpāṇi baśyanti pītāni pītabarṇṇāni pītanidarṣanāni pītanirbhāsāni tadyathā karṇṇikārapuṣpaṃ samānnaṃ bā pārāṇaseyaṃ bastraṃ pītaṃ pītabarṇṇaṃ pītanidarśanaṃ pītanirbhāsamebamebādhyātmamarūpasijñī bahirdhā rūpāṇi baśyanti bītāni bītabarṇṇāni pītanidarśanāni pītanirbhāsānīdaṃ caturthamabhibhvāyatanaṃ. adhyātmamarūbasaṃjñī bahirdhā bāṇi paśyanti lohitāni lohitabarṇṇāni lohitanidarśanāni lohitanirbhāsāni tadyathā bandujībakapuṣpa sampannaṃ bā bārānseyaṃ bastraṃ lohitaṃ lohitabarṇṇaṃ lohitanidharṣanaṃ lohitanirbhāsamebamebādhyātmarūpasaṃjñī bahirdhā rūpāṇi paśyanti lohitāni lohitabarṇṇāni lohitanidarśanāni lohitanirbhāsānīdaṃ pañcamamabhibhvāyatanaṃ. adhyātmamarūpasaṃjñī bahirdhā rūpāṇi baśyantyabadātāni abadātabarṇṇāni abadātanidarśanānya abadātanirbhāsaṃni tadyathā uśanastārakāyā barṇṇa abadāto'badātabarṇṇoñabadātanidarśanoñabadātanirbhāsa ebamebādhyātmarūpasaṃjñī bahirdhā rūpāṇi paśyantyabadātānyabadātabarṇṇānyabadātanidarśanāni abadātanirbhasānīdaṃ ṣaṣṭhamamabhibhvāyatanaṃ. sa sarbaśo rūbasaṃjñānāṃ samatikramātpratighasaṃjñānāmastaṃgamaṃ nānātvasaṃjñānāmamanasikārādanantamākāśamityākāśānantyāyatanamupasaṃpadya biharatīdaṃ sabtamamabhibhvāyatanaṃ. sa sarbasakāśānantyāyatanaṃ samatikramyānantabijñānamiti bijñānānantyāyatanamupasaṃbadya biharatīdamaṣṭamamabhibhvāyatanaṃ.

Names of the ten spheres of totality: nīlakṛtsnāyatanaṃ, hitakṛtsnāyatanaṃ, lohitakṛtsnāyatanaṃ.

Perceiving form inwardly, one looks at small outer forms, of good color and of bad color; having overpowered those forms one knows them, having overpowered them one sees them, and one comes to perceive in that way: this is the first sphere of mastery.

Perceiving oneself inwardly as formless, one looks at outer forms that have become great, of good color and of bad color; having overpowered those forms one knows them, having overpowered them one sees them, and one comes to perceive in that way: this is the second sphere of mastery.

Perceiving oneself inwardly as formless, one looks at outer forms that are blue, blue in color, showing as blue, and giving off blue light. It is just as, for example, a flax flower, or a fine cloth from the land of Vārāṇasī, is blue in color, shows as blue, and gives off blue light; in the same way, perceiving oneself inwardly as formless, one looks at outer forms that are blue, blue in color, showing as blue, and giving off blue light: this is the third sphere of mastery.

Perceiving oneself inwardly as formless, one looks at outer forms that are yellow, yellow in color, showing as yellow, and giving off yellow light. It is just as, for example, the color of the karṇikāra, or a fine cloth from the land of Vārāṇasī, is yellow, yellow in color, shows as yellow, and gives off yellow light; in the same way, perceiving oneself inwardly as formless, one looks at outer forms that are yellow, yellow in color, showing as yellow, and giving off yellow light: this is the fourth sphere of mastery.

Perceiving oneself inwardly as formless, one looks at outer forms that are red, red in color, showing as red, and giving off red light. It is just as, for example, the bandhujīvaka flower, or a fine cloth from the land of Vārāṇasī, is red, red in color, shows as red, and gives off red light; in the same way, perceiving oneself inwardly as formless, one looks at outer forms that are red, red in color, showing as red, and giving off red light: this is the fifth sphere of mastery.

Perceiving oneself inwardly as formless, one looks at outer forms that are white, white in color, showing as white, and giving off white light. It is just as, for example, the white color of the star Venus is white in color, shows as white, and gives off white light; in the same way, perceiving oneself inwardly as formless, one looks at outer forms that are white, white in color, showing as white, and giving off white light: this is the sixth sphere of mastery.

Having completely transcended perceptions of form in every way, with perceptions of resistance subsided, and through not attending to perceptions of variety, thinking, "Space is infinite," one fully attains and abides in the sphere of infinite space: this is the seventh sphere of mastery. Having completely transcended the sphere of infinite space in every way, thinking, "Consciousness is infinite," one fully attains and abides in the sphere of infinite consciousness: this is the eighth sphere of mastery.

The sphere of totality of blue. The sphere of totality of yellow. The sphere of totality of red. The sphere of totality of white. The sphere of totality of earth. The sphere of totality of water. The sphere of totality of fire. The sphere of totality of wind. The sphere of totality of space. The sphere of totality of consciousness.

Some perceive, "The totality is earth," above, below and across, as non-dual and immeasurable. Some perceive, "The totality is water, fire, wind, blue, yellow, red and white," above, below and across, as non-dual and immeasurable.

Emptiness. Signlessness. Wishlessness.

One should rely on the meaning; one should not rely on the letter. One should rely on the Dharma; one should not rely on the person. One should rely on wisdom; one should not rely on consciousness. One should rely on the sūtras of definitive meaning; one should not rely on the sūtras of provisional meaning.

Wisdom arising from hearing. Wisdom arising from reflection. Wisdom arising from meditation.

The science of language. The science of logic. The inner science. The science of healing. The science of the arts and crafts.

The four ways of taking up phenomena: pleasant in the present, ripening later as suffering; painful in the present, ripening later as pleasure; pleasant in the present and ripening later as pleasure too; painful in the present and ripening later as suffering too.

The riches of faith. The riches of moral discipline. The riches of a sense of shame. The riches of regard for others. The riches of learning. The riches of generosity. The riches of wisdom.

Unsurpassable seeing. Unsurpassable hearing. Unsurpassable gain. Unsurpassable training. Unsurpassable service. Unsurpassable recollection.

The foundation of truth. The foundation of relinquishment. The foundation of peace. The foundation of wisdom.

The nine phenomena preceded by supreme joy. In one who is supremely glad, joy arises. The body of one whose mind is joyful becomes thoroughly pliant. One whose body is thoroughly pliant experiences bliss. The mind that has become blissful settles in evenness. When the mind is settled in evenness, one fully knows things just as they truly are, and sees them just as they truly are. Seeing them just as they truly are, one becomes weary of them. Being weary, one becomes free of attachment. Having become free of attachment, one becomes liberated.

When one is liberated, there arises the seeing of wisdom that thinks, "I am liberated."

The six elements of deliverance. Deliverance from malice is the absence of malice, that is, loving-kindness. syābyāpādoniḥsraṇaṃ maitrī. bihiṃsāssaraṇaṃ karuṇā. aratinissaraṇaṃ muditā. kāmaniḥsraṇam upekṣā. bicikitsānissaraṇam asmimānasamutghātaḥ. nimittanaṃḥsraṇam animittaṃ.

Names for the four wheels of gods and humans. Deliverance from harmfulness is compassion. Deliverance from discontent is joy. Deliverance from desire is equanimity. Deliverance from doubt is the complete destruction of the conceit that thinks, "I am." Difficult urination. Dropsical, or with water in the belly. The sprinkling kind of eunuch. A churn. A churning rope. A rafter. A pack. Broad.

The Vyutpatti is complete.

May all beings fare well!

This concludes the translation. The following are translator's notes.

This is the Mahāvyutpatti, a Sanskrit and Tibetan lexicon; the block print alternates runs of Sanskrit with runs of the corresponding Tibetan, and that order has been kept, so some entries break across a run (for example, "One who speaks of nonduality," and "Giver of the supreme"). The two Tibetan renderings "Dispeller of Wrong Faith" and "Destroyer of Saṃsāra" correspond to the two Sanskrit spellings given for the buddha Krakucchanda. The heading before the buddha ground reads "sangs rgyas kyis dang," evidently a slip for "sangs rgyas kyi sa dang" ("the buddha ground and"), which the following entry confirms. The buddha name rendered Meghasvara is the Sanskrit given in the list, not the Meghanāda of the epics.  This piece of the Mahāvyutpatti keeps the block print's alternation of Sanskrit runs and Tibetan runs, so some entries break across runs. The lists given are the eighteen unshared qualities of a buddha (continued from the previous piece) and the thirty-two aspects of the Tathāgata's great compassion; in the latter, the Tibetan frames a series of laments ("Alas, this world...") as what the Tathāgata sees.  This piece of the Mahāvyutpatti keeps the block print's alternation of Sanskrit runs and Tibetan runs, so some entries break across runs. The entries on the six superknowledges are the stock canonical description of the powers of miraculous display; the Tibetan gives the first of them in the first person ("I vanish from upon my seat") and leaves the rest without a stated subject, rendered here with "one." "Just as one does on the great earth" reads dper nas as dper na sa ("for example, the earth"), as the Sanskrit confirms.  This piece of the Mahāvyutpatti keeps the block print's alternation of Sanskrit runs and Tibetan runs, so some entries break across runs; the Tibetan for the final Sanskrit run follows in the next piece. "Body well rounded" renders sku 'khril bag chags pa, where bag chags does not have its doctrinal sense of habitual tendency but describes the compact, rounded form of the body, matching the Sanskrit vṛttagātra.  This is the Mahāvyutpatti, whose Sanskrit and Tibetan lines alternate in the layout, so several phrases are split across blocks. The opening "spread" completes "Forehead well" from the previous piece. This lexicon sets a Sanskrit column beside its Tibetan equivalents, and in this piece the two columns are interleaved, so a few names are split across the break (for example "the nāga king" before one Sanskrit block, with the rest of the name after it). A Tibetan entry cut by a Sanskrit block is given whole where it begins. Lone syllables such as པ that finish a Tibetan word are treated as part of that word. The opening "Jaw" (འགྲམ) completes the Tibetan for Siṃhahanu begun at the end of the previous piece. The second of the four ways of conquest, པས་སུ་པ, is read as བསུས་པ, "coming out to meet", which matches pratyudyāna. In the cakravartin passage, ཆད་པ means "punishment" (daṇḍa), not "annihilation". In the heading on ranks, མེའི་རིམ་པ ("ranks of fire") is read as མིའི་རིམ་པ, "ranks of people", and the illegible character marked X before rāṃjā is left out. The heading of the Pāṇḍava section ends with the unclear phrase མིང་གོ་མཐུན. Some names and titles are uncertain. Sātavāhana (མཐར་འགྲོ་ཞོན) and Karakarṇin (ལོ་ཏང་ཉན) are taken from the parallel Sanskrit column. Hastiniyaṃsa is rendered by the meaning of its Tibetan, "Elephant Tamer". The Tibetan for sthapati, བཙུན་མོའི་ཞལ་ལྟ་བའམ་ཕྱ་མཁན, is rendered tentatively. ཁྲོམ་གྱི་རུ ("leader of the market troop", for bhaṭabalāgra) and ཤོར་བའམ་བཀྲབས་པ (rendered "soldier", after the Sanskrit bhaṭṭa) are also uncertain. ཇོ་བོའམ་རྗེ renders ārya as "lord, or master".  As throughout this lexicon, the Sanskrit and Tibetan columns are interleaved. A Tibetan entry cut by a Sanskrit block is given whole where it begins: "Shoemaker, or leatherworker"; "Cutter of staffs"; "Hired man, or servant, or bondsman"; "Husband and wife"; "Father, or papa"; and "Face, or countenance, or mouth". The phrase "when not rendered literally ... when rendered literally" (མ་འདོམ་ན ... འདོམ་ན) is the lexicographer's note on how a term is translated. In the note on caṇḍāla, གདོས་པ is read as གདོལ་པ, "outcaste". A few Tibetan occupational terms are uncertain and are rendered tentatively. རྒྱས་འཚོར་བ is rendered "netter", reading རྒྱ, "net". ལྟོ་མཁན is rendered "stonecutter", after the Sanskrit śilākuṭṭa. རང་རྟ is rendered "courier". བྱ་མ་རྟ is rendered "scout". The compound འབངས་སམ་ནང་བྱན་ནམ་རྩེ་རྒོད, set against kalpikāra, is rendered "subject, or household attendant, or menial".  This lexicon (the Mahāvyutpatti) is laid out in the source in alternating blocks of Sanskrit and Tibetan, and a few words are split across the break between blocks: the Tibetan "body ... covered with protruding veins," "Bamboo ... Grove," and the name rendered Śarāvatī ("reedy"). Each such word has been translated once, where its second half stands; the split Sanskrit runs are kept in their places as written. In the old-age list the Tibetan has "leaning on saṃsāra" ('khor ba), an evident slip for 'khar ba, "staff," matching the Sanskrit daṇḍa; "staff" is translated. Similarly "dpa' klong" is read as dba' klong, "billows." The rendering "poison-bearer" for the ocean (Sanskrit sāgara) reflects the Tibetan's etymology sa-gara, "with poison." Three Tibetan glosses are uncertain: "hollow of the ear" (rna shal, for camasa), "worn with years" (nas gzer ba, for mahallaka), and "youngster" (bus pa, for lāḍika).  As in the preceding pieces, the source alternates blocks of Sanskrit and Tibetan, and several entries are split across a block break (for example "bringing under control," Mūrdhaṭaka, "the equality of all phenomena," "the Great Illuminator," Yamāntaka); each is translated once, where its second half stands, and the split Sanskrit runs are kept in place as written. The Tibetan for Sanskrit māsakī reads "bdag gi ma," the standard rendering of Māmakī, and is so translated. "Clear vision" (gsal snang) renders prasena, a form of divination in which a deity is made to appear in a mirror or similar medium. The Tibetan "rin chen char 'bebs sam" and "dga' ba chen po sa tshim pa" appear slightly corrupt; they are rendered by their evident sense, "she who rains down jewels" and "she who is sated with great joy."  As before, the source alternates blocks of Sanskrit and Tibetan, and entries split across a block break (for example "pouring ladle," "paralyzing," "with fangs terrifying and hideous," "non-observation") are translated once, where their second half stands; the split Sanskrit runs are kept in place as written. The Tibetan equivalents of the last Sanskrit block fall in the next piece. In "harm from stepping over" the Tibetan reads bsgoms ("meditated"), evidently for bgoms ("stepped over"), matching Sanskrit durlaṅghita. Three renderings are uncertain: "ledge, or rim" (kha ran nam kha khyer) and "parapet" (pu shu), parts of the maṇḍala palace whose pairing with the Sanskrit vedikā and upāṃśu is unclear; and "a staff-like appearance" (breng der snang ba, for daṇḍabhāsa), an astrological omen. Viṣṭi and vyatīpāta are inauspicious periods and conjunctions in Indian astrology. The logical terms in the first part of that section are the five characteristics of a valid proof and their contraries, as taught in the Yogācāra treatises.  As in the surrounding pieces, Sanskrit and Tibetan alternate in blocks, and entries split across a block break (for example "gradually and simultaneously," "inference that arrives by elimination," "the perception definition," "not made by a person," "demerit," "intuitive awareness," "it both has an end and has no end") are translated once, where their second half stands; the split Sanskrit runs are kept in place as written. The Tibetan "srog" that closes the last Tibetan block begins the entry "the life principle is the body," which is completed in the next piece and is translated there. The heading for the Vaiśeṣika categories is likewise split, with a stray mark in the source at the break; it is rendered in its two parts. The Tibetan for laya, "zhab," is obscure; "dissolution" follows the Sanskrit. The fourteen undeclared matters are the questions the Buddha set aside unanswered (avyākṛta). The Mīmāṃsā definition of perception and the Vaiśeṣika examples of inference are given as the lexicon quotes them.  As in the surrounding pieces, Sanskrit and Tibetan alternate in blocks, and entries split across a block break (for example "the life principle is the body," "complete instigator," "the self abides in the formations," the bahuvrīhi compound, the Revatī graha, the female elephant) are translated once, where their second half stands; the split Sanskrit runs are kept in place as written. In Sanskrit grammar the "first person" (prathamapuruṣa) is the third person of European grammar and the "highest person" (uttamapuruṣa) the first. A few Tibetan glosses are evidently miswritten and are translated by their plain sense: "ba lang" and "ba glang mo," which stand for the elephant terms kuñjara and kariṇī; "bye ba" for byi ba, "mouse"; and "glang po ches srung gi bu" for the elephant Airāvaṇa. The Tibetan for śarabha, "ldang ko ska bse kha sko," is garbled, and the Sanskrit name is given. After "koka," the words "tsa mtsha' mme" are unintelligible and are left untranslated.  This lexicon is printed in interleaved Sanskrit and Tibetan columns, so a few words are split across blocks (for example the Tibetan for "Blistering," "the Sāṃkhyas," "hitting by sound," "contest of strength" and "the sound of music," and the Sanskrit sāṃ-khyā, śabda-vedhaḥ, bāhuvyāyā-maḥ, vā-dyaṃ). Each joined Tibetan word is translated where its second half stands, and the Sanskrit fragments are kept where they fall. Several Tibetan bird names look like copyists' slips and are translated by the evident reading: དང་སྐྱ and དང་མོ as ངང་སྐྱ "pale goose" and ངང་མོ "goose," and དུར་པ as ངུར་པ "ruddy goose"; likewise བར་མེ for the note madhyama is read as བར་མ "middle." The birds བྱ་བང (twice), སྡེ and ཐིང་རིལ, and the insect སྲིལ, cannot be identified with confidence and are given by their Tibetan names. In the list of the fields of knowledge, ཐོས་པ and དྲན་པ render śruti and smṛti, revealed scripture and remembered tradition, not mindfulness. In the crafts list the Tibetan again writes གྲངས་ཅན (the Sāṃkhyas) for Sanskrit saṃkhyā, "number"; it is translated as written. The Tibetan for the note ṛṣabha, དྲང་སྲོང "sage," reflects a reading of the name as ṛṣi.  As in the preceding pieces, the interleaved Sanskrit and Tibetan columns split some words across blocks; each joined Tibetan expression is translated where its second half stands (for example the heading "terms that agree in meaning," "cannot be turned back," "not very near," "distracting, or scattering," "wearing armor," "as lovely as a painted picture," "proponent of consciousness only," "endowed with the supreme fullness of a fine complexion"). The first two Tibetan items, "the peaceful" and "dance," gloss śāntaḥ and tāṇḍavaḥ at the end of the previous piece. The Tibetan horse and human sacrifices are written རྟ་གཙང and མི་གཙང, apparently for the usual གསོད "slaughter"; the sense is unaffected. In this lexical context ཕུང་པོ is "heap" and རྒྱུ is the verb "moves about," not "aggregate" and "cause." མ་མོ་འཛིན renders mātṛkādhara, a holder of the mātṛkā, the Abhidharma matrices, not of mother goddesses. རྙིང་པ "old," glossing ullapanaṃ, and སོ་རྩིག, glossing hanyata, are obscure and are translated as written. བདེ་བའི་མཆོག for saṃvaraḥ reflects a reading of the word as śaṃvara, "supreme bliss."  As in the preceding pieces, the interleaved columns split some words across blocks; each joined Tibetan expression is translated where its second half stands (for example "utterly victorious," "small chasm," "rock cave," "marshy," "timely rainfall," "scattered," "thoroughly drawn out," "thoroughly harmed," "hates," "despicable desires," "caught up in coming and going," "whatever is, or which"), and the Sanskrit fragments are kept where they fall. In the heading on the heavens the Tibetan འདོང་བ is read as འདོད་པ "desires," which the list that follows confirms; and ཅེ་སྤྱང་མོའི་སྐྲ "hair of the she-jackal" is read as སྒྲ "cry," matching Sanskrit śivārutaṃ. The gloss འགྱུར་བའམ་འཇུག་པའམ་ལོང་པ for varttate is uncertain in its last member. In this lexical context ཡུལ is "region," ཆད་པ "punishment," and ལམ་འགོག་པ "blocking the road," not their doctrinal senses.  As in the preceding pieces, the interleaved columns split some words across blocks; each joined Tibetan expression is translated where its second half stands (for example "however, or nevertheless," "wherever," "household dwelling," "house roofed with planks," "hall of service," "railing, or coping," "most beautifully adorned"), and the Sanskrit fragments are kept where they fall. The isolated syllable that opens the piece is the tail of a word split by this layout and carries no sense on its own, so it is not rendered. Some glosses are obscure as written: སྒྲ "sound," glossing paṭakuṭi (a cloth hut), and ཤུགས in the gloss on kośa (probably ཤུབས "sheath") are translated as they stand or by the evident reading. In this lexical context གཟུངས་གཟེར is a retaining pin and ཕུར་བུ a peg, not a dhāraṇī or a ritual dagger, and the "Victors of the past" are earlier buddhas.  As in the preceding pieces, the interleaved columns split some words across blocks; each joined Tibetan expression is translated where its second half stands (for example the heading on chariots and implements, "white pea," and the plant glossing valomoṭaḥ), and the Sanskrit fragments are kept where they fall. Many entries are plant and food names; where the Tibetan name can be matched with confidence it is given in English, and where it cannot (the tsitse, srede and chitse grains, the memo or dreu plant, the lidongra plant, the thabe seed, likshiber, the zurpa root, the habshang tseu plant, the myangtsi tré plant, the pore putse shel stone) it is given in simple phonetics. The gloss བྱེ "sand" for satuḥ is read as ཕྱེ "flour." The Tibetan for spring, summer and winter quarters does not exactly match the Sanskrit seasons (hot season, rains, winter); it is translated as written. In this lexical context བདུད་རྩི is the nectar of immortality (amṛta), and ཡུངས་ཀར and སྐེ་ཚེ are the mustard seeds of the house glossary.  This piece belongs to a bilingual Sanskrit–Tibetan word list whose printed lines alternate between a run of Sanskrit headwords and a run of their Tibetan equivalents, so words are sometimes broken across a line and resumed after the next run; each such Tibetan item is rendered whole where it is completed. Several Tibetan equivalents are rare terms of material culture that cannot be identified with confidence and are given in phonetics or by their literal sense: klam, the pachar garment, baglepa cloth, the grukya vessel, the bögya, kakari, dünlé, dozen beads, tölekor, the tsedren crest and chongtsong. The item read "starlight gem" is written sgar 'od, taken as skar 'od, "starlight", as the neighbouring entries require. "Flower radiance" renders me tog 'od as written; as the equivalent of a word for a head chaplet it may be a slip for me tog thod, "flower chaplet". The three entries rendered "Silver" are so written in the Tibetan, although the first of the Sanskrit words they gloss usually means gold.  As in the preceding pieces, the printed lines alternate between runs of Sanskrit headwords and runs of their Tibetan equivalents, so some words are broken at a line end and finished after the next run; each Tibetan item is rendered whole where it is completed, and the sandalwood entry begun at the end of this piece ("that follows the seasons") is completed in the next one. Some Tibetan weapon names cannot be identified with confidence and are given in phonetics: the tewu kanya blade and the tewu kama arrowhead, both apparently types of broad, adze-like heads. The Tibetan rendered "Combat gear" is simply 'thab, "fighting", given twice. The heading rendered "Names of expressions in discourse" reads skad go 'dud, which is obscure; it introduces the stock phrases of a sūtra's opening scene. The entry rendered "Hung down" reads rab tu dbyangs pa, taken in the sense required by its Sanskrit headword.  This section of the word list gives the stock phrases of a sūtra's opening scene and narrative, with Sanskrit runs and their Tibetan equivalents on alternating lines, so a few entries are broken across a line and are rendered whole where they are completed. The Tibetan equivalent of praguṇaḥ reads dang po, "first", which does not fit its headword; it is taken as drang po, "straight". In "trains them, brings them in, and settles them thoroughly", the last verb reads rab tu 'gong, taken in the sense "establish" required by the headword.  The Sanskrit and Tibetan runs of this stock-phrase list alternate line by line, so some entries are broken and are rendered whole where completed; the Tibetan for the last Sanskrit run of this piece comes in the next one. The Tibetan "voice of six thousand qualities" (dbyangs yan lag drug stong) is translated as written, though its Sanskrit headword appears to count sixty. The entry rendered "Set out without touching anything at all" reads ci la yang ma reg par ches pa; the last word is unclear and is taken as chas pa, "set out", to fit its headword, which means "girded and ready".  As before, the Sanskrit and Tibetan runs alternate line by line, so a few entries are broken across lines and rendered whole where completed; the short Tibetan gloss "the Jewel, or a precious thing" stands inside a Sanskrit line and is translated in place. A few Tibetan equivalents are hard to construe and are rendered literally: "like the sun that has passed beyond the mountain" (its Sanskrit headword speaks of gold freed from rock), "having made it accord with the other's arrow", and the string of alternatives "wrapped all around, or unwiped, or unwashed, or knotted, or parted".  This is a section of the Sanskrit-Tibetan lexicon: runs of Sanskrit headwords alternate with runs of their Tibetan equivalents, and a few words are split across the runs by the page layout; these are given whole here (jyotirgaṇaḥ, bhyāmīkaraṇaṃ, kilāsaḥ, yathartukam, pāramparyaṇa, with their glosses likewise joined). The single Tibetan gloss "remains waiting" serves both āgamayamānaṃ tiṣṭhati and tiṣṭhati pratīkṣamāṇaḥ. The Tibetan equivalents of the last Sanskrit run fall in the next piece. Several evident copyist's slips in the Tibetan are translated by their plain readings: "wait!" for sdong cing (sdod cig); "not clinging" for chags pa (ma chags pa, matching aśleṣaḥ); "ordinary" for bal ba (phal ba); "unhurried" for me rings pa (mi rings pa); "unsoiled" for gas pa med pa (gos pa med pa); "ascetic" for dge spyod (dge sbyong); "a quarter" for gzhi char (bzhi char). Here 'phags pa glosses prasthāna and means "rising up," not "noble one." The equation of "having cleansed stinginess" with avalambhanam is unclear, and the Tibetan is rendered as it stands.  This piece is part of a Sanskrit and Tibetan word list; the Sanskrit lines and their Tibetan equivalents alternate and break mid-word at line ends, which the rendering follows. The Tibetan word ཁྱེའུ in the gloss on āṇī...nirhārayogena is taken in its sense of "peg" or "wedge" (the classic image of driving out one peg with another), not "boy". The gloss on śūra is compressed and its sense, that the rendering depends on whether the word is paired with vīra, is reconstructed. "Writing tablet" reads མི་གེ་ཤིང as ཡི་གེ་ཤིང, and "meteor" reads སྐང་མདའ as སྐར་མདའ, both evident copyists' slips. The isolated Tibetan phrase སྐད་གོ་འདུན་ལ standing among the Sanskrit entries is obscure and its rendering as a heading is tentative, as is "a flare" for ཏལ་ལ. "Sinks" and "rises" render the colloquial Tibetan expressions ནེ་མ and སྤར.  As in the surrounding pieces, Sanskrit lines and their Tibetan equivalents alternate and break mid-word at the line ends; the English follows those breaks. Three evident copyists' slips are read in their plain sense: དུ་བ ("smoke") as ངུ་བ, "weeping", matching prarodana; དབང་པོ as དཔང་པོ, "witness", matching sākṣin; and ཡང་བ in the gloss on viṣamāparihāra taken in the sense of གཡང་བ, "precipice, dangerous ground". The phrase ཆེ་བཞི་རྨས་བ in the gloss on sākṣipṛṣṭa is obscure and is rendered loosely as "formally interrogated"; the gloss on pratoda and ཁྱེའུ་ཚང ("nest") under pratyagra are likewise uncertain.  As throughout this word list, the Sanskrit lines and their Tibetan equivalents alternate and break mid-word at line ends; the English follows those breaks, and the piece ends on a Sanskrit line whose Tibetan equivalents begin the next piece. The Tibetan gloss given for mṛtagṛha, ཆད་པ, does not obviously match the Sanskrit ("house of the dead"); it is rendered literally as "cut off". The gloss "well roasted" for suparidharjitā and "abandoned" for varṇṇavāśī likewise translate the Tibetan as it stands, though the correspondence with the Sanskrit is unclear.  As throughout this Sanskrit and Tibetan word list, the lines break mid-word, and the English follows those breaks; the opening Tibetan glosses belong to Sanskrit entries given at the end of the previous piece. Two Tibetan glosses are read with evident copyists' corrections: རྣམ་པར་མྱུར ("utterly swift") for vipariṇata is read as རྣམ་པར་འགྱུར, "changed", and རྒྱུད་པ for vipatti as རྒུད་པ, "decline". In the gloss on pradālitā, གཤེགས་པ is taken in the sense of གཤགས་པ, "split". The paired-word notes ("when not paired ... when paired ...") are the compiler's remarks on which Tibetan rendering to use depending on whether the Sanskrit synonym stands alone or together with another.  As throughout this Sanskrit and Tibetan word list, the lines break mid-word and the English follows those breaks; the piece ends on a Sanskrit line whose Tibetan glosses open the next piece. In the gloss on anantarajanma, མཆོད་པའི་ཚེ ("the life of worship") is read as མ་ཆོད་པའི་ཚེ, "the life not cut off", that is, the immediately following life; ཆགས་ཞེ་ན is read as ཆགས་ཞེན, "attachment and clinging". The gloss སྐོ་ཤམ་པ for vedhi is obscure and is rendered tentatively as "allotted fate". The weaving terms སོན་པ, ཐགས་བཟང and ཚིག་པ are rendered by the plain sense of the Sanskrit they gloss (bobbin, loom, weaver's brush), since the Tibetan words are not clear in this sense. ཤེད་བྱེ་བ in the gloss on vyutpanna is taken as "with abilities unfolded", not as the name Manu.  As throughout this Sanskrit and Tibetan word list, the lines break mid-word and the English follows those breaks. The Tibetan names in the list of story subjects are standard renderings: ཁར་བ for Kaṃsa, རོལ་སྙེད for Sītā and གསེར་ལྡན་མ for Rukmiṇī. The remarks "when paired with ..." are the compiler's notes on which Tibetan rendering to use when these Sanskrit words for "cause" occur together.  This piece begins the word list's section on the names of large numbers, first those of the chapter on numbers of the Avataṃsaka, then those of the Gaṇḍavyūha. The Tibetan names of most of these numbers are coinages without a usable literal meaning (many are reduplicated syllables), so each is rendered by the Sanskrit number name it stands for in the parallel Sanskrit list, in the form this text gives; because the two lists here differ from each other, the same Tibetan word is sometimes matched to different Sanskrit names, as the source has it. Where the Tibetan names are meaningful (incalculable, immeasurable, boundless and so on), they are translated, and the Tibetan "added to itself" after each is rendered as "squared". The gloss on lobhayat is obscure and is rendered literally.  As in the preceding pieces, the Tibetan number names, mostly coinages, are given by the Sanskrit names of the parallel column; the Tibetan of the asaṃkhyeya series is translated, with "las bsgres pa" rendered "squared". The Tibetan "bsgres ga mu med" is read as "bsgres pa, mu med", and "rdzogs thob phyag brgya" as "phrag brgya". The heading of the Lalitavistara section stands in the Tibetan just before the last word, "squared", of the preceding entry; that word is placed with its entry here.  As before, Tibetan number names that are coinages are given by the Sanskrit names of the parallel column, while plain numerals and descriptive terms are translated. The Tibetan "tshang med pa" is read as "tshad med pa", immeasurable. In the worldly list the Tibetan gives "bye ba", ten million, twice, against the Sanskrit koṭī and arbuda. For samudra the Tibetan gives two names, "thig 'bum" or "mtsho yas"; the second is translated.  The Tibetan note on rendering kāla, velā and samaya, "ma 'dom na ... 'dom na", is obscure and is translated by context. The five Tibetan terms from "dusk" to "before dawn" render the five praharas, or watches, of the Sanskrit. The Tibetan "tsher bas" is read as "tshe rabs", future lives; "spyid ba" as "spyod pa"; and "pas pham pa" as "phas pham pa", defeat. The Tibetan month names are lunar-mansion names; they are given here as the Sanskrit months they designate.  These are the names of the monastic offenses; the Tibetan entries are terse headwords, rendered as such. The Tibetan "zas gsod pa" is read as "zas bsod pa", fine food; "mi 'bud pa" as "me 'bud pa", kindling a fire; and "dad pa phyir bsgyur ba" as "'dun pa phyir bsgyur ba", retracting consent, in line with the Sanskrit chanda. An asterisk in the Tibetan after "seeking a begging bowl" is disregarded.  These are the headwords of the monastic training rules, rendered tersely. The syllables "ku la" at the head of the piece, continuing "cotton" from the preceding piece, are unclear and are kept as written. The entry "folded like a palm leaf" lacks the negative in both columns, though the rule forbids it. The Tibetan "min bar" is read as "mi na bar", not ill; "lag pa mi blta ba" as "lag pa mi ldag pa", not licking the hand; and "drung na 'dug pa'am dge slong" as "the monk sitting nearby".  The two Tibetan syllables "mi na bar", not ill, that open the piece belong to the first Tibetan sentence and are translated there. The Tibetan "ba shags" is read as "bshags", to be confessed; "gzhi nas spong ba" as "gzhi nas spo ba", probation started over; and "dad pa dang yongs su dag pa" as "'dun pa dang yongs su dag pa", consent and purity. "Medicine of fermented urine" renders the Sanskrit pūtimukta; the Tibetan says literally "medicine smeared and discarded".  These are the impediments to going forth listed in the Vinaya. The Tibetan gives two words, "smad 'chal" and "zhar ba", for the single Sanskrit entry kāṇḍarikaḥ kāṇaḥ. The Tibetan "ya za ma lug", opposite tālamukta, is obscure and is given by the Sanskrit; "gta' gam pa" is rendered conjecturally as one held in pledge. "Sha mdog sde ches pa" is read as skin too dark, after the Sanskrit nīla. Tibetan "ser ba" in this list means yellow, not hail.  Many of these requisites are rare words; the Tibetan is rendered by its plain sense, guided by the Sanskrit where the Tibetan is unclear, and some identifications are tentative. The Tibetan "sdas nang tshangs can" is read as "sngas nang tshangs can", a stuffed pillow; "skra'i rkang rten" as "khri'i rkang rten", supports for the bed legs; and "sdeng spyad" as "snod spyad", utensils. This piece belongs to the Mahāvyutpatti lexicon and preserves the page layout, in which lines of Sanskrit and of Tibetan alternate and sometimes break in mid-word (for example the Tibetan for the Mahāsāṃghika school and for the poṣadha basis are split across lines); the translation follows that order. The heading reads literally "for the forty-seven"; bzhi is evidently a slip for gzhi, "the seventeen bases," which is what the list that follows gives. "The school of the clan that proclaims on the earth" renders the Tibetan for the Kaurukullaka school literally; the Tibetan rendering is not transparent. "The basis of the conduct of one moved to another standing" (Skt. bhūmyantarasthacaraṇa) most likely refers to the probationary status (parivāsa). "Woollen garment" reads mal gos as bal gos, matching the Sanskrit aurṇaka; similarly dre gtsang khang is read as dri gtsang khang, the fragrant chamber, and dge spyod as dge sbyong, renunciant. The cloth names be'u ras, la dmar and be'u phrug are rendered approximately. The final section gives the technical definitions used for the first two defeats (sexual intercourse and theft) and the third (taking human life). In the final sentence the Tibetan reads dad pa'i, "of faith"; the Sanskrit chanda shows that 'dun pa'i, "of consent," is meant, and it is so translated. Other evident slips read as intended: dad bcas for dang bcas ("fraught with suspicion"), tha mal for tha ma ("first and last"), mnyams pa for ma nyams pa ("unimpaired"), and 'ug for nyug ("stroking"). The Tibetan phrase skad go 'dun la, standing among the Sanskrit entries with no Sanskrit counterpart, is obscure, and its rendering is tentative. This is an AI-generated translation from the AI Stupa project. It was produced by a careful, doctrine-aware process aiming at a high degree of accuracy, but it has not been reviewed by a human translator and may contain errors. It is offered as a provisional translation for those texts of the Tibetan Buddhist canon that have not yet received an authoritative human translation. Where a published translation exists — above all from 84000: Translating the Words of the Buddha (84000.co) — that translation is definitive and should be preferred; please consult it instead. These translations are released into the public domain (CC0) in the spirit of spreading the Dharma to all corners of the earth.

Just as the Bodhi tree now standing at Bodh Gayā neither is nor is not the same tree beneath which the Buddha attained awakening, so too this stupa is intended as the next generation of stupa — dependently arising according to the conditions of its environment, and ceaselessly proclaiming the Dharma for the benefit of all sentient beings.

May whoever encounters these words derive great benefit.
